- [Compose accessibility guidance](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/accessibility/merging-clearing) says a clickable parent merges child text into one accessibility node; [Android's accessibility principles](https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/ui/accessibility/principles) advise against repeating child text in a group description. The carousel's real clickable `Card` previously added the title as `contentDescription` even when no caller description was supplied, risking a repeated or incomplete announcement when supporting text was present. It now lets the native card merge its visible title and supporting text by default. When `AdaptiveCarouselItem.semanticDescription` is supplied, that explicit description replaces the text semantics while the card keeps its native click action. Actual TalkBack speech and focus order remain device checks.
- Still to verify on a device: image crop and mask transitions, click semantics on small items, uncontained end behavior, RTL, large text, callback indices, default edge padding and custom tap timing.

## Flexible top app bar heights

- The current [Compose app-bar guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/app-bars) and [Material insets guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/system/material-insets) show the top bar handling system insets and the scaffold passing content padding to the body. They do not prescribe an extra fixed margin below the bar. Source review and Flutter geometry tests confirm the host reserves the bar's measured height plus the top inset; the title's visual baseline and any apparent missing space in the reported screenshot still need inspection on a real Android device before changing global body spacing.
- Official sources: [`TopAppBarDefaults`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/TopAppBarDefaults), [`MediumFlexibleTopAppBar`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/MediumFlexibleTopAppBar.composable), and [`LargeFlexibleTopAppBar`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/LargeFlexibleTopAppBar.composable).
- Small bars with supporting text previously placed a two-line `Column` in the title slot of the no-subtitle `TopAppBar` or `CenterAlignedTopAppBar`. The pinned Compose Material 3 artifact provides a [`TopAppBar` subtitle overload](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/TopAppBar.composable) with separate title/subtitle slots and horizontal alignment. A nonblank subtitle now uses that native overload, keeping the official subtitle typography and placement; an absent subtitle keeps the existing small-bar overload. The Flutter host still measures 64 dp with a small subtitle, as verified by a focused widget test. Native text layout and spacing await an Android device check.
- Flutter reserved the taller subtitle height whenever `subtitle` was non-null, including empty or whitespace-only text. Compose used `isNullOrBlank()` and rendered the shorter bar. This could leave a strip of unused height below the native bar. The Flutter host now uses nonblank text as the condition, matching Compose. The native bar now reads expanded and collapsed heights from `TopAppBarDefaults` rather than local literals. The Flutter host still mirrors the current token values because Scaffold needs its preferred size before the native view is measured. Inspection of the pinned Material 3 1.5.0-rc01 artifact confirms 112/136 dp for medium flexible and 120/152 dp for large flexible, without/with subtitle.
- A widget test passed for empty, whitespace-only, and visible subtitles in both flexible variants; the capped Kotlin compile passed. Device checks remain for status-bar inset, scroll collapse, title wrapping at large text scales, and the absence of a blank strip below the bar.
- A focused Flutter geometry test now supplies a 24 dp top system inset and confirms that the native small bar occupies 88 dp and the body begins at 88 dp, both with and without bottom navigation. This verifies the `Scaffold`/platform-view boundary in Flutter; an Android device is still needed to verify Compose's title baseline and visual space below its text.
- The native app bar previously called `FocusRequester.requestFocus()` on composition, even without a keyboard entry event. If the leading button was disabled and the first enabled action fell into the overflow menu, its requester was not attached to a visible control and could fail. The forced request and requester modifiers are removed; the real Compose buttons and overflow trigger retain their normal visual order, and disabled controls are skipped by native focus traversal. [Compose's focus traversal guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/touch-input/focus/focus-traversal) describes Tab moving through visible controls, while [programmatic focus guidance](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/touch-input/focus/request-focus) reserves explicit requests for a user flow. Kotlin compilation passed; physical-keyboard entry, overflow traversal, and TalkBack still need a device check.
- The current [Compose app-bar examples](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/app-bars) use `TextOverflow.Ellipsis` for titles and subtitles constrained to a line count. The native bridge had used `Clip`, which could sever a long label at the edge without indicating more text. Its small app bar now ellipsizes after one line; flexible bars retain two-line wrapping and ellipsize only when that limit is exceeded. Caller-supplied title `TextOverflow.visible` or `ellipsis` still takes precedence through the serialized text options. Native layout with long localized titles and large font sizes remains a device check.
- App-bar action disabled state: `AdaptiveAppBarAction` previously serialized every action as enabled, leaving its Compose `IconButton`/`TextButton` disabled state unreachable; the overflow `DropdownMenuItem` ignored that property too. The public action now exposes `enabled` with a true default and forwards false to both direct and overflow controls. [Compose `IconButton`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/IconButton.composable) and [`DropdownMenuItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/DropdownMenuItem.composable) provide the native disabled appearance, input suppression and accessibility state. Flutter app-bar fallbacks and the existing iOS toolbar's `UIBarButtonItem.isEnabled` follow the same flag. A focused test failed on the absent API, then passed all 23 cases including stale Android event suppression and fallback input suppression; targeted Dart analysis, Swift parse, and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation passed. Device appearance, overflow touch and focus remain pending.
- A later audit found one exception: the action placed inside `AppBarWithSearch` used a real Compose `IconButton` but ignored the serialized `enabled` flag, leaving a focusable, rippling control whose Flutter callback was suppressed. It now passes `enabled` to that native button. The search-field action and app-bar overflow trigger also use the official [`TooltipBox`/`PlainTooltip` pattern](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/IconButton.composable), matching the existing leading and ordinary actions. All app-bar icon tooltips now carry localized pane titles and assertive live-region semantics from the current Compose sample. Capped Kotlin compilation passed; hover, long press, keyboard focus and TalkBack still need an Android device.
- The native overflow menu sent `actionPressed` in the same click that set `expanded = false`, so an app navigation could dispose the app bar before the real popup exit. The pinned AndroidX [`DropdownMenu` source](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/material3/material3-android/1.5.0-rc01/material3-android-1.5.0-rc01-sources.jar) keeps its popup while the transition's current or target state is expanded. The bridge now queues one action until its menu content leaves composition, then sends it through the latest callback; the overflow trigger cannot reopen during that exit. Each app-bar action also carries a weakly held ID, allowing Flutter to resolve a delayed event against the current enabled action list after reordering or removal. A focused reorder test failed before the ID bridge and all 24 app-bar-action tests passed afterward. Native exit timing remains a device check.
- Scroll bridge review: the official [app-bar scroll guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/app-bars) applies the `TopAppBarScrollBehavior` to consumed content motion. Flutter previously sent only the last scroll delta observed before each build. Multiple notifications could coalesce into one platform-view update, causing `enterAlways` to miss part of the motion in both the regular and search variants. The bridge now sends the absolute scroll offset; Compose calculates each delivered offset difference and updates its real `TopAppBarState` or `SearchBarScrollState`. The focused Flutter app-bar test and capped Kotlin compilation pass; on-device scroll motion remains pending.
- Flutter now also forwards scroll start, end, and whether content is at its start. On an end signal, the native regular/flexible top bar follows the [AndroidX `settleAppBar` implementation](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/frameworks/support/+/c73a560c5c9ba4ed53a4f6dd5df36dfd16a837e7/compose/material3/material3/src/commonMain/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/AppBar.kt): `AnimationState` animates its real `TopAppBarState.heightOffset` to the nearer endpoint using `TopAppBarDefaults.snapAnimationSpec`. A new scroll cancels the snap. The flexible `exitUntilCollapsed` bridge now advances from the current height with delivered deltas and only expands at the content start, so a later gesture does not jump back to its old absolute-offset height. A focused Flutter drag test and capped Kotlin compilation pass; native frame-by-frame behavior remains unverified.
- The search variant now settles a partially hidden collapsed bar at scroll end. [AndroidX `settleSearchBar`](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/frameworks/support/+/d8461b2e660905fa3e6485612b90af7aa8da332e/compose/material3/material3/src/commonMain/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SearchBar.kt) chooses the nearer endpoint at half the offset and uses the default effects motion token. The bridge applies that rule to the real `SearchBarScrollState` with `MaterialTheme.motionScheme.defaultEffectsSpec<Float>()`; a new scroll cancels the animation. Expanded search is excluded. The capped one-worker Kotlin compile passed; device motion remains unverified.
- The shared Compose platform-view box centered every component vertically. A collapsing top app bar therefore moved away from the status edge inside its fixed-height host, creating empty space above as well as below it. The top app bar is now aligned to the host's top edge; other components retain their existing alignment. The body-space problem is addressed by the measured-height bridge described below.
- The Flutter `Scaffold` previously reserved the app bar's expanded height throughout a native collapse. The real Compose app bar now reports its measured height via [`onSizeChanged`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/ui/layout/onSizeChanged.modifier), and Flutter updates `PreferredSize` so the body follows that height. The platform view remains measured at the expanded height inside a clipped `OverflowBox`: Compose can re-expand even while Flutter currently exposes only the collapsed portion, and the clipped region does not take body hits. A widget test first reproduced the 48 dp stale body gap after a synthetic native collapse, then passed for collapse and re-expansion while the platform view retained its full measuring height. This is still an asynchronous cross-framework layout bridge and can lag by a frame; clipping and gesture behavior need Android device verification.
- Title-widget parity: the official [`TopAppBar` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/TopAppBar.composable) accepts a composable title slot. `AdaptiveAppBar.titleWidget` promises to replace the plain title, but the native bridge had prioritized `title` and silently discarded a `Text` widget's style. It now gives `titleWidget` precedence, forwards supported explicit text properties through the shared Compose `Text` serializer, preserves heading semantics and the variant's line limit, and uses Flutter `AppBar` for unsupported fonts/effects. A focused test failed red on the wrong title before passing; six app-bar tests, targeted Dart analysis and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation passed. Large-font layout and accessibility remain device checks.
- A second bridge test reproduced a stale event race: after changing a medium flexible bar to a large flexible bar, an old native height event shrank the new bar to the previous 88 dp. The Android platform view now has a key derived from variant, scroll behavior, subtitle presence and top inset, so a geometry change disposes the old channel and starts a fresh native bar. The test now confirms that the late event cannot resize the new 144 dp host.
- The Flutter scaffold also reset its saved offset whenever the parent supplied a new `body` widget instance, even if the `Scrollable` element and controller retained their position. This could expand an `enterAlways` bar and reset its scrolled color on an unrelated parent rebuild. The reset now occurs only when the bottom-navigation selected index changes. A widget regression test passed after rebuilding the body while its list stayed at 300 dp.
- Integration limit: Flutter's [ScrollNotification guidance](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/widgets/ScrollNotification-class.html) says these notifications arrive after layout, so `setState`-driven layout follows one frame later. Compose's [app-bar guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/app-bars) attaches the scroll behavior's `nestedScrollConnection` to a Compose `Scaffold`; that connection cannot directly observe an arbitrary Flutter `Scrollable`. The current bridge lacks native fling velocity/decay, and the measured-height event adds another asynchronous frame boundary. On-device motion and body placement must be compared before claiming full native scroll behavior.

## Bottom navigation bar

- A native navigation-bar click previously sent only the old destination index through the platform channel. If the host app reordered destinations before delivery, `AdaptiveScaffold` could invoke `onTap` for a different tab. Each serialized destination now has a weakly held ID; both real Compose tall and short navigation items send that ID with the index, and Flutter resolves it against the current destination list. Removed destinations are ignored. The focused reorder/removal widget test failed before the ID bridge and all seven bottom-navigation tests pass after it; runtime event ordering still requires a device.
- The native compact and expanded navigation rails had the same index-only event across flattened sections. Both official Compose rail item variants now send the destination's weak ID; the Flutter bridge searches its current flattened list and ignores a removed item. The two-variant reorder/removal test failed before the bridge and all eight rail tests pass afterward. Real click delivery remains to verify on a device.

- Official sources: [M3 navigation bar overview](https://m3.material.io/components/navigation-bar/overview), [Compose `NavigationBarItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationBarItem.composable), and [`NavigationBarItemDefaults.colors`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationBarItemDefaults).
- The scaffold omitted the entire Android bar when `onTap` or `selectedIndex` was null, despite the public API documenting a visible, noninteractive bar and an optional selection. When rendered, the native item used a click callback guard rather than Compose's disabled state, so it still had interactive ripple and accessibility state. Dart also omitted `alwaysShowLabels` while native code defaulted to false, so `hideLabels: false` showed only the selected label. `unselectedItemColor` never reached Compose.
- Current change: render the Android bar for any nonempty destination list; pass `enabled` to the native item; show all labels when `hideLabels` is false; send the unselected color to Compose's icon and text roles. When labels are visible, the icon's own content description is null to avoid repeating the visible label in accessibility output. The existing documented `selectedItemColor` mapping to indicator color is preserved for compatibility. The test-only Flutter Material fallback chooses the first destination when no index is supplied because Flutter's `NavigationBar` cannot represent an unselected index.
- The [official Compose navigation guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/navigation-bar) still recommends `NavigationBar`/`NavigationBarItem` for three to five destinations on compact windows, so this remains a supported native Material 3 route. [ShortNavigationBar](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ShortNavigationBar.composable) is a separate expressive option with `EqualWeight` on small screens and `Centered` on medium screens; its [items](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ShortNavigationBarItem.composable) use top icons on compact widths and leading icons on medium widths. AndroidX's official [`NavigationBarTokens`](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/frameworks/support/+/androidx-main/compose/material3/material3/src/commonMain/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/tokens/NavigationBarTokens.kt) assign a 64 dp short container and an 80 dp tall container. `AdaptiveAndroidNavigationBarStyle.short` now exposes the real Compose short bar with those responsive layouts and matching Flutter host height; the existing tall style remains the default for existing apps.
- The native bridge forced the same `surfaceContainer`/`onSurface` pair for both styles and `0.dp` tonal elevation for the tall bar. The official [`NavigationBar`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationBar.composable) and [`ShortNavigationBar`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ShortNavigationBar.composable) publish separate defaults. The tall bar now uses `NavigationBarDefaults.containerColor`, `contentColorFor(containerColor)`, and `NavigationBarDefaults.Elevation` unless explicitly overridden; the short bar uses its own `ShortNavigationBarDefaults` colors. This restores native theme token behavior without changing the Dart-facing color override contract.
- Still to verify on a device: disabled appearance and TalkBack state, label density on three to five destinations, badge announcements, contrast of caller colors, and system navigation-bar inset placement.
- Public custom-bar contract: `AdaptiveBottomNavigationBar.bottomNavigationBar` promises to replace the generated Android bar. The Android `AdaptiveScaffold` page without generated `items` omitted that property entirely, so a caller's custom bar disappeared. That route now forwards it to Flutter's [`Scaffold.bottomNavigationBar`](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/material/Scaffold/bottomNavigationBar.html), which places it below the body and reserves its space. The focused widget test failed on the missing bar before the fix and passes with the custom bar mounted and the body ending at its top; the two native generated-bar tests also pass. This affects only the caller-supplied Flutter bar, not the real Compose generated tall/short bars.
- [AndroidX's official `BadgedBox` navigation sample](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/BadgedBox.composable) gives a count badge a contextual `contentDescription` rather than leaving a bare number. `AdaptiveNavigationDestination.badgeSemanticsLabel` now lets callers supply that localized description. The native tall and short bars apply it to the real Compose badge text without replacing the destination name. Cupertino and Android Flutter fallback badges receive the same value; the latter previously omitted the badge entirely. Six focused navigation tests pass, including both selected and idle Cupertino icon states. A device TalkBack check is still needed to confirm how the item merges its label and badge announcement.
- The [official Compose navigation guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/navigation-bar) makes `NavigationBarItem.label` optional and gives its icon a content description. The package's tall and short native bars previously passed an empty `Text` slot whenever a destination label was blank; a bare icon then had no accessible name. Both real Compose item variants now pass `label = null` for blank text. The Flutter bridge accepts an icon-only destination only when the normal and selected icon states have nonblank `Icon.semanticLabel` names, respecting the existing selected-style fallback when `selectedIcon` is not an `Icon` widget. The native icon slot keeps the supplied description, while visible-label items still avoid duplicate icon speech. The invalid-name regression failed before the guard; all 12 focused bottom-navigation tests, full Flutter analysis, the complete 833-test suite and capped offline Kotlin compilation pass. TalkBack order and actual label-slot geometry still need a device.

- Duplicate-destination follow-up: a destination object reused twice in the bottom bar received one ID in both slots, so a tap on the second slot resolved to index 0. The shared per-occurrence allocator now serializes distinct IDs and resolves taps against the current list. The duplicate test failed before the fix; the bottom-bar, carousel and button-group suites now pass together (32 tests). Native click timing and focus remain device checks.

## Navigation rail and drawer

- Official sources: [Compose navigation drawer guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/drawer), [Compose predictive Back setup](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/system/predictive-back-setup), [`ModalDrawerSheet` with `DrawerState`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ModalDrawerSheet.composable), [`WideNavigationRailItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/WideNavigationRailItem.composable), and [`NavigationRailItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationRailItem.composable).
- The modal drawer's native sheet received `Modifier.fillMaxSize()`, overriding the package's requested width and Compose's drawer width limit. Its overlay also treated `destinationSelected` as nonterminal, so selecting a destination could leave the native dialog route alive above Flutter. Selection and dismissal events were delivered before the native close animation. The rail's optional FAB had no accessibility name, and visible rail labels repeated the icon's content description.
- Current change: pass the requested drawer width across the bridge and constrain the native `ModalDrawerSheet` to Compose's maximum; declare destination selection terminal on the Flutter overlay; provide `DrawerState` to the modal sheet for predictive Back; wait for the native close state before reporting selection or dismissal. The drawer sheet and scrim now use `DrawerDefaults` color tokens. Both rail styles use their Compose default container color unless the caller overrides it. Rail FABs now require an action callback to render and accept a localized description. Rail destination icons have no duplicate content description when a label is visible.
- The same [Compose `BadgedBox` accessibility pattern](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/BadgedBox.composable) used by the bottom bar also applies to navigation rail items. Both native compact and expanded rail styles now forward `AdaptiveNavigationRailDestination.badgeSemanticsLabel` to the real Compose badge text. The Flutter rail fallback now renders its existing string badge on both selected and idle icons and passes the localized description to `Text.semanticsLabel`. The focused bridge and fallback tests pass; announcement order still needs a device TalkBack check.
- The official [Compose `FloatingActionButton` example](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/FloatingActionButton.composable) wraps a FAB in `TooltipBox`/`PlainTooltip` with a pane title and assertive live region. Both native rail variants now share that whole-FAB tooltip, using the rail's localized `fabContentDescription` when supplied. Compose still owns the real FAB, its ripple and motion. Long press, hover, focus and TalkBack announcement need device verification.
- The current [`NavigationRailItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationRailItem.composable) and [`WideNavigationRailItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/WideNavigationRailItem.composable) APIs accept a null label slot. The package's compact and expanded rails instead sent an empty `Text` for blank destination labels, and optional blank `semanticLabel` could override visible text with an empty accessible description. Both real Compose item variants now omit their label slot when text is blank and attach the required localized `semanticLabel` to the icon. The Dart bridge rejects unnamed icon-only destinations and explicit whitespace-only semantic overrides. The real rail FAB likewise requires a nonblank contextual `fabContentDescription` when clickable, so it no longer falls back to an enum identifier such as `add`. The two regressions failed before their guards; all 15 focused rail tests, the full 836-test Flutter suite, full Dart analysis and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation pass. Native geometry and TalkBack remain to verify on a device.
- The official [Compose accessibility semantics guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/accessibility/semantics) marks visual section titles with `heading()` so screen readers can navigate between them. Native compact and expanded rail section headers now carry that semantics property. In the Flutter rail fallback, the destination's existing `semanticLabel` now reaches its `Text` label, including Flutter's hidden-label semantics for compact rails. The focused fallback test failed on the missing name and then passed; native heading traversal still needs TalkBack verification.
- The official [Compose navigation drawer guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/drawer) places trailing badge content in a real `NavigationDrawerItem`; the [Material badge example](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/BadgedBox.composable) gives that content a contextual description. Drawer destinations now accept optional `badgeSemanticsLabel` and pass it to the native item's badge text. The disabled item's merged description uses the same contextual phrase instead of a bare count. The Flutter drawer fallback also forwards the badge description and now uses `selectedIcon` on the selected destination, matching the native route. Two focused tests first failed on the absent field; all five drawer tests, targeted Dart analysis and capped Kotlin compilation passed. Actual TalkBack announcement remains a device check.
- The pinned AndroidX `NavigationDrawer.kt` constrains `ModalDrawerSheet` to a 240 dp minimum and [`DrawerDefaults.MaximumDrawerWidth`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/DrawerDefaults). The package previously used a responsive 88%-of-screen width for the visible drawer widget but a fixed 304 dp width for the Flutter gesture placeholder and native modal overlay, so a 320 dp screen reserved 281.6 dp in one path and 304 dp in the others. All three now derive the same width from the current `MediaQuery`, bounded to 240–360 dp. Explicit caller widths retain their existing bounds. Focused tests reproduced the modal mismatch and cover the host, placeholder and `AdaptiveScaffold` wiring at 320 and 800 dp; device sheet placement remains to verify.
- The original Flutter rail host fixed its compact width at 80 dp and expanded width at 256 dp unless the caller supplied `expandedWidth`. The native rail previously filled only the host height, so its surface could leave part of a wider host uncovered. Both Compose rail variants then filled the host's measured size. This uses the official [`WideNavigationRail` modifier](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/WideNavigationRail.composable) and [`NavigationRail` modifier](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationRail.composable); actual surface bounds still need an Android device check, particularly with custom `expandedWidth`.
- Dynamic expanded-width audit: the pinned AndroidX `WideNavigationRail.kt` measures its direct items and computes an expanded width between its 220 dp minimum and 360 dp maximum with the default spatial spring. The package's fixed 256 dp host constrained that native measurement and could clip long labels. With no explicit `expandedWidth`, Flutter now mounts a native view up to 360 dp wide, clips only the space it reserves, and follows Compose's reported width without a second animation or view recreation. Compose's real rail stays aligned to logical start and no longer fills that oversized measurement viewport. A focused host test failed at 256 dp and passes with a 360 dp native viewport and 320 dp reported host; all ten rail tests and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation pass. Native paint, item measurement during scroll, and RTL placement still require a device.
- Width-measurement follow-up: after a destination label changes, Flutter temporarily exposes the full native viewport so longer content cannot remain clipped while Compose remeasures. The rail now tags each width report with the visual-content key; Flutter ignores late reports from an earlier label. Compose republishes its latest measured width when that key changes, even if the size itself remains the same. One new test reproduced the old 220 dp clipped host after changing the label; a second verifies clipping from the logical start on a 200 dp RTL window. All 12 rail tests, capped Kotlin compilation, and full Dart analysis pass. Native label transitions and RTL paint still need a device.
- The wide rail state was remembered before branching on compact versus expanded, so a view switched from compact to expanded could retain the `Collapsed` initial state. It is now created only in the expanded branch. The official [`rememberWideNavigationRailState`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/rememberWideNavigationRailState.composable) uses its argument as an initial value. [`WideNavigationRailItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/WideNavigationRailItem.composable) normally shows every supplied label; for the package's explicit `selectedOnly` option, unselected items now omit the label and give their icon the accessible name. On the Flutter fallback, an extended `NavigationRail` requires `labelType: none` and places labels beside icons itself; the fallback now uses that valid combination and forwards `expandedWidth` as `minExtendedWidth`. Flutter's extended rail therefore always shows its labels even for `selectedOnly` or `neverShow`; honoring those options there would require a different Flutter layout. Device checks remain for rail changes, label transitions and accessibility.
- The pinned AndroidX [`NavigationRail` source](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/material3/material3-android/1.5.0-rc01/material3-android-1.5.0-rc01-sources.jar) spaces direct children by 4 dp. The package groups destinations in a scrolling `Column`, which Compose sees as one direct child; compact destinations had no gap. The nested column now applies the same 4 dp spacing. The native bridge also forwarded out-of-range selection values unchanged while the Flutter fallback clamped them. Both paths now select the same in-range destination. The four focused rail tests and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation pass; item spacing still needs a device check.
- The compact `scrollable: false` route still used that nested column even though it had no scroll modifier. It now emits its section headings and `NavigationRailItem`s directly, letting the real `NavigationRail` apply its own child spacing and layout. Only `scrollable: true` retains the nested column and its matching 4 dp gap. Capped one-worker Kotlin compilation passed; native placement still needs device inspection.
- The pinned [`WideNavigationRail` layout](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/material3/material3-android/1.5.0-rc01/material3-android-1.5.0-rc01-sources.jar) measures each direct destination separately to calculate expanded width and vertical placement. The package's `scrollable: false` route still wrapped every destination in one `Column`, defeating that measurement. That route now emits direct `WideNavigationRailItem` children. `scrollable: true` retains the scrolling column, so its per-item native measurement remains a known compromise until the scrolling behavior can be redesigned and checked on-device. Capped Kotlin compilation passes for the new direct-child route.
- Rechecked the exact pinned 1.5.0-rc01 sources against the current [official `WideNavigationRail` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/WideNavigationRail.composable). The composable exposes no scrolling parameter; its private layout measures each direct child and animates expanded item spacing to 0 dp. The package's scrolling `Column` is therefore one measured child. For the default top arrangement its own zero-gap items approximate the native vertical placement, but the rail does not measure or arrange those destinations individually. Moving `verticalScroll` to the outer rail would scroll the header and surface together, so it is not a faithful fix. Preserve the current explicit `scrollable` behavior until a device-backed layout solution is available; the non-scrolling route keeps direct native children.
- Compose's `NavigationDrawerItem` still lacks an `enabled` parameter in the pinned library. A no-op callback and reduced opacity alone left a native click target and ripple. Disabled destinations now keep the actual `NavigationDrawerItem` for geometry and color, but consume pointer events on the initial pass before the item's click handler, remove keyboard focus, and replace its accessibility semantics with its label, badge, selection, and disabled state. The approach follows [Compose's pointer event passes](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/touch-input/pointer-input/understand-gestures) and [`clearAndSetSemantics`](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/accessibility/merging-clearing). Device checks remain for disabled hover/ripple/TalkBack behavior, actual sheet width, scrim/Back/gesture close, predictive Back, rail labels and FAB focus.
- Width finding: pinned [`ModalDrawerSheet`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ModalDrawerSheet.composable) limits its content to 240–360 dp, while Flutter previously reserved any explicit `width`. A 200 dp host was narrower than the native minimum and a 500 dp host left empty space beside the 360 dp sheet. The Flutter host, scaffold placeholder and overlay property now bound explicit width to the same 240–360 dp range. A focused widget test reproduced the 200 dp host before the fix and passes at 240/360 dp. Native items now read [`NavigationDrawerItemDefaults.ItemPadding`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationDrawerItemDefaults) instead of a 12 dp literal, and the header has Compose heading semantics. Kotlin compilation passed; device sheet geometry and TalkBack remain open.
- Narrow-window follow-up: the 240 dp floor still made a 200 dp split-screen window reserve more drawer width than was available. The Flutter gesture placeholder, native host and modal property now cap the requested width at the current window width; Compose no longer reapplies the floor after that cap. The pinned AndroidX 1.5.0-rc01 `NavigationDrawer.kt` source applies `Modifier.sizeIn(minWidth = 240.dp, maxWidth = DrawerDefaults.MaximumDrawerWidth)` inside `ModalDrawerSheet`; the caller's preceding `Modifier.width(requestedWidth)` and a narrow window's constraints can still constrain the actual sheet below the preferred minimum. This is an inference from modifier order; visual placement remains a device check. Both automatic and explicit narrow-width tests failed before the correction, then all 11 drawer tests and capped Kotlin compilation passed. [Android's adaptive navigation guidance](https://developer.android.com/develop/adaptive-apps/guides/build-adaptive-navigation) recommends choosing navigation presentation from the current window size.
- End-drawer direction audit: the overlay reversed `LocalLayoutDirection` around the complete `ModalNavigationDrawer` to place the sheet on the opposite edge. In the pinned AndroidX `NavigationDrawer.kt`, the outer drawer reads that direction to place and drag the sheet, and `ModalDrawerSheet` reads it for predictive Back. The inherited reversal also reached the header, text, badges and `NavigationDrawerItem` start/end padding, so an LTR app's right drawer laid out its content as RTL (and vice versa). The sheet now receives the original app direction only inside its content slot. The real drawer/sheet keep the reversed direction for side placement and Back motion. [Compose's layout-direction guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/layouts/custom#layout-direction) documents the scope of `LocalLayoutDirection`. The pinned Kotlin module compiles; visual placement, swipe and predictive Back in both LTR and RTL need an Android device.
- The pinned AndroidX `NavigationDrawerItem` source assigns `Role.Tab` through its real `Surface`, so the package's disabled-item replacement semantics retain that same role. The official [`NavigationDrawerItem` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationDrawerItem.composable) requires a `label` slot; a whitespace-only destination label previously produced an unnamed native control. The public drawer now checks each destination before building an inline view, a Scaffold placeholder or a modal overlay and reports the invalid index. The focused test failed on the previous behavior and all 12 drawer tests passed after the Dart guard. This guard also covers the Flutter fallback.
- The same official [`NavigationDrawerItem` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/NavigationDrawerItem.composable) makes its trailing `badge` slot optional. A whitespace-only badge previously supplied a real but empty slot, reserving trailing layout space without visible content; optional header/supporting strings similarly reached the native property bridge as blank values. The Dart bridge now sends null for all blank optional drawer text and omits an orphan badge description when there is no badge. Native Compose also ignores a blank raw badge before passing its `badge` lambda to the real item. The Flutter fallback omits matching header, subtitle and trailing widgets. The native-property regression failed before normalization; both new tests and all 15 focused drawer tests, full Dart analysis, the complete 838-test suite and capped Kotlin compilation pass. Native spacing still needs device inspection.
- The [official Compose drawer guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/drawer) uses real `ModalNavigationDrawer`, `ModalDrawerSheet` and `DrawerState` to animate a close. The package already used those controls, but its delayed selection retained only an index. If the Flutter destination list changed during the native close, the callback could select a different destination. Each native item now carries a weakly held identity; after the close, the bridge resolves it against the current `AdaptiveScaffold` drawer and ignores removed or disabled destinations. The inline native drawer applies the same enabled guard. Two focused tests first reproduced a disabled callback and a reordered modal selection, then all nine drawer tests passed. Android close timing still needs a device check.

- Duplicate navigation audit: compact/expanded rails flatten all sections into one selection index, but a destination object reused in two sections previously received one native ID. The second item's tap resolved to the first. The rail now allocates IDs for the flattened occurrence sequence and serializes them back into their sections. The modal drawer had the same per-object ID problem; it now uses occurrence IDs for both the live native view and the delayed overlay result lookup. Both duplicate tests failed before the change; all 19 rail/drawer tests and targeted Dart analysis pass afterward. Native click delivery and drawer exit timing still require a device.
- Drawer width boundary audit: the [official Compose drawer guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/drawer) confirms that the package uses its real `ModalNavigationDrawer`, `ModalDrawerSheet`, and `DrawerState`; [`DrawerDefaults.MaximumDrawerWidth`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/DrawerDefaults) sets the native upper width token. The existing 240–360 dp cap remains intact, including a narrower available window. An explicit non-finite `AdaptiveNavigationDrawer.width` was silently converted by Dart's clamp instead of reported to callers. The public bridge now rejects that invalid width before mounting an Android view or opening the overlay. The new test failed before the validation and all 13 drawer tests passed afterward; full Dart analysis passed. The change is Dart-only and preserves native Compose drawer animation.

## Slider

- Official sources: [Compose Material 3 `Slider`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/Slider.composable), [`rememberSliderState`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/rememberSliderState.composable), [`SliderDefaults.colors`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SliderDefaults), and [native thumb `Label`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/Label.composable).
- The bridge used the deprecated value-based slider overload. Its thumb drew from Flutter's last property value, which could lag the live drag, and it emitted a custom text line above the slider when dragging. That line shifted the track and could exceed the fixed 64 dp Flutter host.
- Current change: use the official state-based `Slider` and `rememberSliderState`, keep local thumb motion responsive while Flutter receives values, and apply externally supplied values outside the drag. The native `Label` and `PlainTooltip` now anchor the optional text to the thumb, with Compose's default press/hover behavior. Without a caller color override, `SliderDefaults.colors()` tokens govern the thumb and track. The Flutter host reserves only the 48 dp slider area because the native label is a popup.
- Width audit: the native slider applied an extra 8 dp horizontal padding inside the width supplied by Flutter. The official [`Slider` example](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/Slider.composable) applies layout padding to a parent and leaves the slider's modifier unpadded. The package now gives the real Compose slider its full host width, so callers can choose any outer spacing in their own layout. Device verification remains for the thumb's visual end positions.
- Callback audit: Compose's [`onValueChangeFinished`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/Slider.composable) closes drags and taps and fires for each keyboard step. The prior bridge emitted `onChangeStart` only from `collectIsDraggedAsState`, so taps and keyboard changes had a finish without a start. Flutter's installed `Slider` calls `onChangeStart` with the previous value before the first change for both taps and keyboard steps. The native bridge now starts on the first `onValueChange`, closes on `onValueChangeFinished`, and pairs callbacks even when a tap finishes without a value change. This keeps the actual Compose slider and its native motion. Device checks remain for gesture cancellation and keyboard sequence timing.
- Empty-range and value-boundary audit: [Flutter's Slider API](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/material/Slider-class.html) disables the control when `min == max`; the Android bridge had still marked its real Compose `Slider` enabled and allowed callbacks. It now forwards disabled state for that case and drops all associated events. The public constructor follows Flutter's range, value and positive-division assertions and also requires finite values because the native [AndroidX `SliderState`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SliderState) stores `Float` values. Passing large, closely spaced Dart doubles directly to that Float range can collapse both endpoints to one value. The Flutter bridge now maps every finite public range to a normalized 0–1 track for the real Compose slider and converts native start/change/end events back to Dart doubles, with exact public endpoints and overflow-safe arithmetic for ranges spanning both extremes of `double`. It rejects nonfinite or out-of-range native events. Three additional focused tests failed before normalization; all 26 slider/selection tests passed afterward. Compose rendering, discrete steps and keyboard behavior still need an Android device.
- Disabled-value parity: [Flutter's `Slider`](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/material/Slider-class.html) disables interaction when `onChanged` is null but retains its `value` for the thumb and track. The Android bridge tied normalization to the callback, sending 0 instead of a valid nonzero value whenever disabled. It now normalizes every valid nonempty range independently of `onChanged`, while `enabled` and event handling remain false without a callback. A focused test failed with a 0.0 native payload before the fix; all 18 slider and range-slider tests pass afterward. The real state-based Compose slider still renders disabled colors and semantics.
- Still to verify on a device: continuous and discrete dragging, external programmatic value updates during/after a drag, tooltip position near screen edges, large text, RTL, hover, and TalkBack value actions.
- The pinned [AndroidX `RangeSlider` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/RangeSlider.composable) includes a state-based two-thumb overload with separate interaction sources and native thumb slots; the package had no public range-slider adapter. `AdaptiveRangeSlider` now wraps that real Compose component on Android. It normalizes finite public double ranges to a 0–1 native track, uses `RangeSliderState` for drag and discrete steps, applies caller colors through `SliderDefaults.colors`, and places optional `Label`/`PlainTooltip` content on each native thumb. Flutter's `RangeSlider` handles other platforms. Five bridge tests cover bounds, normalized values including the full finite double range, event validation and disabled empty ranges; the capped one-worker Kotlin compile passed. Native overlapping thumbs, keyboard/TalkBack adjustment, RTL, label positioning and external updates during a drag still need device checks.
- Disabled range parity: Flutter's [RangeSlider contract](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/material/RangeSlider/onChanged.html) disables input when `onChanged` is null but retains `values` as the displayed range. The Android bridge had tied serialized start/end to the enabled flag, so disabling a nonempty selection moved both native Compose thumbs to zero. It now sends the normalized selected range whenever `min < max`, independently of enabled state; an empty track still uses its disabled zero position. The focused test reproduced the old 0.0 payload, then all six range-slider tests passed. Compose still owns the disabled colors, semantics and thumb rendering; an Android device is needed to inspect them.
- Accessibility source audit: the pinned [`RangeSlider` implementation](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/material3/material3-android/1.5.0-rc01/material3-android-1.5.0-rc01-sources.jar) creates separate start/end thumb semantics nodes, gives them localized descriptions, and attaches progress semantics and keyboard handlers before invoking our thumb content. The package's optional `Label` slots therefore leave those native handlers intact. Flutter 3.47.9's own `RangeSlider` reports percentages by default, so the normalized Compose track preserves that default announcement pattern. Custom semantic value formatting is not exposed yet; actual TalkBack wording, focus order and step size remain device checks.
- Discrete callback precision: the [1.5.0-rc01 release](https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/compose-material3#1.5.0-rc01) fixes native tick snapping, but Compose still transfers each selected `Float` through the platform channel. Converting a 0.3 tick directly to Dart `double` yielded `0.30000001192092896` in both slider adapters. The Flutter bridge now rounds only discrete callbacks to the caller's exact tick index before denormalizing to the public range; continuous callbacks retain their original precision, and min/max still map exactly. This changes no Compose component or native animation.

## Button groups

- Official sources: [Compose `ButtonGroup`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ButtonGroup.composable), [`ButtonGroupScope.animateWidth`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ButtonGroupScope), [`ToggleButtonDefaults.contentPaddingFor`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ToggleButtonDefaults), and the [M3 button-group guide](https://m3.material.io/components/button-groups/overview).
- Geometry boundary follow-up: caller `spacing` reached both Flutter's first-frame width/height estimate and Compose `Arrangement.spacedBy` without a finite check. A NaN gap produced a generic `BoxConstraints(NaN)` failure before the Android platform view was mounted. `AdaptiveButtonGroup` now rejects nonfinite spacing with a field-specific error; finite negative spacing remains supported for the connected overlap. The focused test failed on the old generic exception, then all 22 group tests passed. No Kotlin component changed.
- Overflow selection style follow-up: `ButtonGroup` itself is still the native AndroidX component, but this package's custom overflow rows used the older `DropdownMenuItem` and only added a manual `selected` semantic. Pinned AndroidX Material 3 `1.5.0-rc01` exposes [`CheckableDropdownMenuItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/CheckableDropdownMenuItem.composable) for multiple selection and [`SelectableDropdownMenuItem`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SelectableDropdownMenuItem.composable) for single selection. The custom rows now use those real Compose controls with `MenuDefaults.itemShapes()`, native selected color/shape transitions, Checkbox/RadioButton semantics, and animated leading-icon replacement. The existing selection-required guard, delayed popup exit, item identity, and disabled state remain in their respective paths. AndroidX does not expose the first overflow index through `ButtonGroupMenuState`, so the default item shape pair is used rather than guessing first/middle/last overflow positions. Capped offline one-worker Kotlin compilation passed; shape and TalkBack behavior still need a device check.
- The native group already uses Compose `ButtonGroup`, `customItem`, `ToggleButton` variants, `ButtonGroupDefaults` connected shapes, and an interaction source shared with `animateWidth`. However, `animateWidth` used its generic compression limit even though the toggle button's padding changes with size, style, and the presence of an icon. AndroidX explicitly recommends passing the end padding of the actual button to avoid excessive neighbor compression.
- Current change: derive the compression limit from the matching filled, tonal, outlined, or elevated native toggle-button `contentPaddingFor(size, hasStartIcon)` and the current layout direction. Compose still owns the width spring and shape morph. The capped one-worker Kotlin compile passed.
- The action's `tooltip` formerly served only as an accessibility-description fallback. It now wraps that action's native toggle button in Compose [`TooltipBox`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/TooltipBox.composable) with `PlainTooltip` and an individual `TooltipState`. To retain the [official `ButtonGroupScope` width animation](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/frameworks/support/+/8b6942263237d29650d16cfff913e0273a9f8ce1/compose/material3/material3/src/commonMain/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ButtonGroup.kt), the group width modifier belongs to the outer tooltip anchor and the focus modifier stays on the inner toggle button. The position provider uses the current `TooltipAnchorPosition.Above` overload.
- The pinned [official connected vertical-group sample](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ButtonGroup.composable) rounds the first action's top corners and the last action's bottom corners. The package had reused horizontal leading/trailing shapes in a vertical `Column`, leaving its rounded ends on the left and right. Vertical groups now derive both resting and pressed native shapes from `connectedMiddleButtonShapes()` with top/bottom ends; a one-action group keeps the native standalone toggle shape. Kotlin compilation, four group tests and full Dart analysis pass; a device is still needed for shape and press-morph visuals.
- Spacing audit: pinned AndroidX `ButtonGroupDefaults.HorizontalArrangement` uses the official 12 dp standard gap and `ConnectedSpaceBetween` uses 2 dp. The previous package default varied standard spacing by button size (18/12/8 dp), bypassing Compose's group token. A null `spacing` now reads the actual Compose defaults. The [official vertical connected sample](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ButtonGroup.composable) uses `Arrangement.spacedBy((-6).dp)` so its stacked buttons overlap slightly; the package had left a positive 2 dp gap. The native vertical route now follows that sample, while explicit caller spacing remains authoritative. Capped one-worker Kotlin compilation passed; device layout and touch overlap remain to verify.
- Single-selection audit: the Flutter fallback could select multiple checked actions even when `selectedIndex` selected one, while Compose chose the explicit index or the first checked action. The bridge now resolves one index before rendering and forwards that same selection to native Compose. Multi-selection still uses the explicit index set or every checked action. Two focused tests first failed on the discrepancy, then passed. The button-group guide now describes the pinned AndroidX default gap accurately: 12 dp at every size, unless `spacing` is supplied.
- The Flutter fallback also chose `checkedLabel` from the action's original `checked` hint instead of the resolved selection. It now uses the same selection for the visible label and checked icon, matching the native Compose button. A focused test failed on the former stale label and passed after correction.
- Overflow focus audit: the pinned [AndroidX `ButtonGroup` source](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/material3/material3-android/1.5.0-rc01/material3-android-1.5.0-rc01-sources.jar) measures and places only the first `visibleItemCount` buttons and moves the rest into a `DropdownMenu`. The package's horizontal arrow handler enumerated all enabled action indices and could request focus on an action Compose had not placed. The [Compose keyboard guidance](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/touch-input/input-compatibility-on-large-screens) says its focusable buttons ordinarily handle Tab and arrow navigation. The horizontal group now leaves that navigation to Compose, including its overflow indicator. The manual Up/Down handler remains for vertical groups, where all actions are laid out. A device keyboard pass is needed to confirm entry, exit, RTL and overflow traversal.
- Host geometry audit: `AdaptiveButtonGroup` mounted an Android platform view without a height. A scrollable Flutter `Column` therefore supplied unbounded vertical constraints; in a bounded `Scaffold` the same group expanded to the full 600 dp test viewport. The host now begins at the pinned native toggle size (32/40/56/96/136 dp, with a 48 dp minimum interactive target), adds every vertical item's height and the native gap, and then follows Compose's `onSizeChanged` report. `Modifier.wrapContentHeight(unbounded = true)` lets Compose report text-wrapped content even before Flutter grows the host. A focused test failed on the old 600 dp host; all 11 group tests, targeted Dart analysis and capped Kotlin compilation pass. Actual overflow height, large text and transient first-frame clipping still require device inspection.
- Width audit: the same host still had no explicit width, so a horizontal Flutter scroller gave `RenderAndroidView` infinite width. A focused test reproduced that assertion before the fix. The host now estimates each toggle's first-frame width from the pinned `ToggleButtonDefaults` size-specific content padding, icon size, icon gap and text role; standard groups sum their actions and Compose gap, while connected groups reserve equal segments. An unconstrained horizontal scroller gets finite width, a bounded standard group remains bounded so [Compose `ButtonGroup`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ButtonGroup.composable) can place extra actions in its native overflow menu, and a bounded connected group fills the row. The native group reports actual width and height to refine its Flutter host. All 12 focused tests, targeted Dart analysis and capped Kotlin compilation pass. Actual RTL/large-text width and overflow layout still need device inspection.
- Icon-only accessibility audit: an action with only an icon could serialize a null `semanticLabel`, leaving the native toggle with an empty content description, and the `ButtonGroup` overflow menu rendered an empty `DropdownMenuItem` text. The bridge now resolves a nonblank name from `semanticLabel`, tooltip, visible label or icon identifier in that order; the native menu uses that name as visible text when there is no label. Authors should supply a localized `semanticLabel` or tooltip for icon-only actions. The focused test first reproduced the missing name, then passed all five cases. Targeted Dart analysis and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation passed; TalkBack and overflow layout still need a device check.
- Native-event audit: Compose `ToggleButton` and its overflow `DropdownMenuItem` already disable inaccessible actions, but a platform-channel selection event can arrive after Flutter shortens or disables the action list. The Flutter bridge now checks the current index and enabled flag before invoking either single- or multi-selection callbacks. A focused test covers invalid and disabled indices in both modes; all 13 button-group tests passed. This changes only the bridge and leaves Compose's native toggle motion and focus behavior intact.
- Overflow exit audit: the package-level `selectionRequired` rule was enforced by visible toggle buttons but not by their native overflow menu, so tapping the last selected overflow item closed the menu without changing selection. Both paths now share the guard. The pinned AndroidX `ButtonGroup.kt` hosts overflow entries in `DropdownMenu`; `AndroidMenu.android.kt` retains that popup until its `MutableTransitionState` finishes exiting. A valid selection is now queued on tap, the real menu dismisses, and a `DisposableEffect` in the menu content notifies Flutter after the popup leaves composition. The official overflow indicator is temporarily disabled while a choice is pending so another tap cannot reopen the menu mid-exit. Device timing, rapid taps and layout changes during exit remain to verify.
- Delayed-selection identity audit: if the Flutter app reordered actions before the real overflow popup finished exiting, the old numeric index could select a different action. Flutter now serializes a weakly held unique ID per action; visible toggles and overflow entries return that ID, and Dart resolves it against the live list before applying the existing enabled and selection-required guards. Removed actions are ignored. A focused reorder/removal test failed before ID serialization and all 15 button-group tests, targeted Dart analysis, and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation pass. Device popup timing remains to verify.
- Accessible-name audit: [`ToggleButton` in the pinned source](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/material3/material3-android/1.5.0-rc01/material3-android-1.5.0-rc01-sources.jar) uses a toggleable `Surface`, which merges its visible `Text`; [Compose's semantics guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/accessibility/merging-clearing) describes that behavior. The bridge nevertheless added a `contentDescription` derived from the original action label or tooltip to every toggle. When `checkedLabel` changed, the accessible name could remain on the old label. Native toggles now rely on their visible text by default and add a content description only for icon-only actions or an explicit caller `semanticLabel`. An unchecked icon-only action with a `checkedLabel` now derives its fallback name from the icon rather than announcing its future selected label. The overflow menu retains a nonempty fallback name. All 14 focused group tests and capped one-worker Kotlin compilation passed; TalkBack speech for checked-label changes remains a device check.
- Compose identity audit: the bridge already gives each action a stable ID for delayed selection events, but the native group's remembered interaction sources, tooltip state, pressed morphs and vertical focus requesters were still associated with list positions. Reordering actions could carry a pressed or focused state to a different action. Per-action Compose `key(id)` now scopes the visible button content; the vertical focus requester list resets when the ordered IDs change. This follows the [official Compose lifecycle guidance](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/lifecycle) for stateful dynamic lists while keeping the real `ButtonGroup`, toggle controls and their animations. Capped offline Kotlin compilation passed with one worker; an Android device is still needed to verify an active press, tooltip and keyboard focus across a reorder.
- Duplicate-action audit: the Flutter ID bridge originally assigned one ID per action object. Reusing a canonical `const AdaptiveButtonGroupAction` twice therefore gave both positions the same ID, so the second native selection resolved to the first and Compose's keyed content had duplicate identities. IDs now belong to each occurrence of an action object, remain stable for its occurrence ordinal across builds, and are looked up from the current ordered list. The new widget test failed with equal IDs before the fix; all 16 button-group tests, targeted Dart analysis and `git diff --check` pass afterward. Identical occurrences are indistinguishable if the app swaps them, so their ordinal position is the only meaningful identity without an explicit application key.
- Read-only state audit: the official [`ToggleButton` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/ToggleButton.composable) uses `enabled = false` to suppress input and expose native disabled colors and accessibility state. When callers supplied no selection callback, the group previously still serialized every action as enabled, so visible buttons rippled and the overflow trigger opened a menu even though Flutter could not change the selection. The bridge now marks actions enabled only when the matching single- or multi-selection callback exists; the official overflow indicator is disabled when no enabled action remains. Flutter fallback chips follow the same rule. The two new single-selection tests failed before the change; 19 focused group tests and capped Kotlin compilation pass afterward. Native disabled colors, overflow focus and TalkBack remain device checks.
- Icon-only content audit: the pinned AndroidX 1.5.0-rc01 `ToggleButton.kt` source inserts `Spacer(Modifier.width(iconSpacing))` whenever its leading `icon` slot is non-null. The package filled that slot for icon-only actions and also supplied `Text("")`, leaving a real but unnecessary gap. Icon-only actions now draw their icon in the actual Compose toggle's content slot at `ButtonDefaults.iconSizeFor(buttonSize.height)`; labeled actions keep the native leading-icon slot and its official spacing. The Flutter host's first-frame width and Compose width-compression padding use the corresponding no-leading-icon geometry. The focused width test failed at 80 dp before the correction and passed at 72 dp afterward. Actions whose unchecked or checked state would have neither text nor icon now fail clearly instead of drawing an empty toggle. All 21 group tests and capped Kotlin compilation pass; actual shape motion and layout still need a device.
- Still to verify on a device: width expansion and neighbor compression with and without tooltips for all five sizes and four styles, long/localized labels, RTL, connected equal widths, overflow menu selection, disabled actions, tooltip placement and trigger behavior, keyboard focus and TalkBack.

## Standalone search field

- Official sources: [Compose `SearchBar`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SearchBar.composable), [`SearchBarDefaults.InputField`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SearchBarDefaults), and the [AndroidX SearchBar source](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/frameworks/support/+/androidx-main/compose/material3/material3/src/commonMain/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SearchBar.kt).
- `AdaptiveSearchBar` exposes a compact editable field and callbacks for changes, submission, tapping and clearing. It has no results content or expanded-search API. Its native view keeps the Boolean-expanded Compose `SearchBar` collapsed and uses the state-based Boolean-expanded `InputField` overload while forwarding a tap to Flutter.
- The newer state-based `InputField` automatically expands `SearchBarState` when it receives touch focus. The newer collapsed `SearchBar` is designed to pair with `ExpandedFullScreenSearchBar` or `ExpandedDockedSearchBar` for results. A direct overload replacement would enter an expanded state without a corresponding result surface and change the public widget's behavior. Keep the current native overload until the package exposes an expanded search contract, or provide a separate explicit field-only API with matching native semantics.
- Rechecked the pinned AndroidX Material 3 1.5.0-rc01 `SearchBar.kt`: its new `InputField(textFieldState, searchBarState, ...)` also expands on a later touch after keyboard focus and on typing, then clears touch focus after collapse. `AdaptiveSearchBar` has neither a results composable nor an expansion callback, so simply replacing the deprecated overload would create an invisible expanded state and change focus/IME behavior. The app-bar search route already uses the state API with a real results surface. The official [SearchBar API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SearchBar.composable) and [release notes](https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/compose-material3) distinguish collapsed bars from expanded search views. This deprecation is a deliberate compatibility limit pending a separate public search-results contract, not a reason to simulate the component.
- The pinned source also provides an intermediate [`SearchBarDefaults.InputField(state: TextFieldState, expanded: Boolean, ...)`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SearchBarDefaults) overload. It retains the collapsed Boolean contract but uses the real state-based `BasicTextField` internally. The standalone search route now uses that overload, so selection and IME composition no longer collapse to a cursor at the end on every native edit. The Flutter bridge exchanges text, selection and composition, and increments an external revision for programmatic controller updates; Compose synchronizes only those external revisions. The clear affordance now follows the live native state. This overload is still deprecated in favor of `SearchBarState`, which requires an expanded results contract the public widget does not yet expose. A focused bridge test failed on missing selection properties before migration; all five search tests, targeted Dart analysis and capped Kotlin compilation passed. Native IME, clear-button isolation and focus need a device check.
- The app bar's search variant already uses the state-based API with an `ExpandedFullScreenContainedSearchBar` and suggestion content. Device checks remain for standalone search focus, tap callback, IME submission, clear action, and the app bar's expanded results.
- App-bar search result audit: the official [Compose search-bar guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/search-bar) uses a `LazyColumn` for potentially long result lists. The package's real `ExpandedFullScreenContainedSearchBar` had placed every suggestion in a non-scrollable `Column`, so large sets could be clipped in the bounded full-screen content region. It now keeps the same native search surface and `ListItem` rows but composes only visible results in a `LazyColumn`. The previous result and IME callbacks were sent before `SearchBarState.animateToCollapsed()`, allowing navigation to dispose the platform view before its native spring finished. A pending event now waits until `currentValue` is collapsed and `isAnimating` is false; it is delivered once against the latest callback, while repeated taps during closing are ignored. The pinned AndroidX source confirms the expanded dialog leaves composition when `currentValue` becomes collapsed; Compose `Animatable.isRunning` is observable snapshot state. Capped offline one-worker Kotlin compilation passed. Long-list scrolling, keyboard dismissal, result timing and TalkBack remain device checks.
- The old `InputField` sends `onExpandedChange(true)` when it gains focus, including programmatic focus. That was wired to `AdaptiveSearchBar.onTap`, so focus could impersonate a tap and later taps on the focused field might not reach the callback. The native route now observes `PressInteraction.Release` from its own `MutableInteractionSource`, with a current callback reference, and sends `tapped` on completed input presses. Focus changes remain a separate event. The official [InteractionSource guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/touch-input/user-interactions/handling-interactions) describes this stream; physical tap and accessibility behavior still need a device check.
- Verified this event path in the pinned Compose Foundation 1.12.0 source jar: `BasicTextField.kt` passes its `interactionSource` into `TextFieldDecoratorModifier`, which calls `detectTextFieldTapGestures` with that same source; `TextFieldSelectionState.kt` emits `PressInteraction.Release` after `tryAwaitRelease()` succeeds and `Cancel` otherwise. The source artifact is `androidx.compose.foundation:foundation-android:1.12.0` from the [official Google Maven repository](https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/compose/foundation/foundation-android/1.12.0/foundation-android-1.12.0-sources.jar). This supports completed press forwarding but does not replace a device check for clear-button isolation or assistive activation.
- Programmatic focus audit: the Android search route accepted a Flutter `FocusNode` but never attached it to a Flutter `Focus` widget. As [Flutter's `requestFocus` contract](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/widgets/FocusNode/requestFocus.html) explains, a detached node defers the request until attachment, which never happened here. The native route now hosts the supplied or internal node with `Focus.withExternalFocusNode`, forwards its focus changes to Compose's real `FocusRequester`, and reflects native focus events back to Flutter. A nonfocusable parent disables descendant focus when `enabled` is false without replacing caller `FocusNode` attributes. Flutter semantics are omitted on the wrapper so Compose remains the field's accessibility owner. Focused tests first failed for detached nodes and then for disabled focus; all search tests now pass. Native IME, keyboard traversal and touch focus still require a device.
- Late-edit boundary audit: Compose's `InputField(enabled = false)` stops new native editing, but a platform-channel `changed` event can arrive after Flutter rebuilds the field as disabled. The Dart bridge already rejected late tap, clear and submit events, yet still accepted that edit and changed the public `TextEditingController`. It now checks the current `enabled` state before decoding or applying native edits. A widget test reproduced the controller mutation after disabling a previously enabled search field, then passed with all ten focused search tests. No Compose control or animation was replaced; delayed IME-event timing still needs an Android device.

## Native accessibility labels and selection

- Official sources: [Compose graphic accessibility defaults](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/accessibility/api-defaults), [Compose `selected` semantics](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/ui/semantics/SemanticsPropertyReceiver), and [Flutter `MaterialLocalizations`](https://api.flutter.dev/flutter/material/MaterialLocalizations-class.html).
- The native app bar overflow, automatic navigation button, standalone search clear button, and icon popup trigger used fixed English descriptions. Their Flutter bridges now send `MaterialLocalizations` labels to Compose; a caller's explicit leading `IconButton.tooltip` still takes precedence. A safe English fallback remains for hosts without Material localizations. The popup icon uses a `Builder` to read localization at the widget's actual build context.
- A custom leading `IconButton` without a tooltip was announced as the localized Back action even when its nested `Icon` supplied a different `semanticLabel` (for example, Close). The native bridge now prefers the explicit tooltip, then the icon's semantic label, then the localized generic navigation label. A focused widget test reproduced the wrong label before the change and passed afterward. [Compose app-bar guidance](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/app-bars) assigns a meaningful `contentDescription` to navigation icons; TalkBack still needs a device check.
- Checked items in the native button-group overflow and split-button menu used a hardcoded "Selected" icon description. Their `DropdownMenuItem` now exposes Compose's `selected` semantics and the checkmark is decorative. This lets Android accessibility services announce the state in their own locale instead of reading a fixed English string.
- Remaining device check: TalkBack and keyboard traversal should announce labels and selected state exactly once in several locales. The standalone search bar still uses the older collapsed Compose overload for the public-contract reason above.

## Segmented controls

- Official sources: [Compose segmented-button guide](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/segmented-button), [`SegmentedButtonDefaults`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SegmentedButtonDefaults), and AndroidX's [outlined segmented-button height token](https://android.googlesource.com/platform/frameworks/support/+/cf0e9fa73871ecd8ba9722ae1bf289e1020f191e/compose/material3/material3/src/commonMain/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/tokens/OutlinedSegmentedButtonTokens.kt).
- The native `SingleChoiceSegmentedButtonRow` uses a 40 dp container, but the Flutter host reserved only 36 dp by default, risking a clipped platform view. Android now reserves at least 40 dp while retaining the existing iOS height. The bridge also used `labels.length` to clamp selection even when an icon-only control had no labels; this threw before the native view could mount. The Dart and Compose sides now count the larger of labels and icons. Without an explicit selected color, Compose's `SegmentedButtonDefaults.colors()` supplies all state colors instead of forcing one active role. The public icon size, icon color, unselected text color and selected text color previously stopped at the Flutter/Compose boundary; they now reach the real Compose control, with `SegmentedButtonDefaults.IconSize` and native color tokens retained when absent.
- [Compose's `SegmentedButton` API](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SegmentedButton.composable) and [`SegmentedButtonDefaults.Icon`](https://developer.android.com/reference/kotlin/androidx/compose/material3/SegmentedButtonDefaults) provide the official icon slot, checkmark and checked/unchecked crossfade. The package had put caller icons in the label slot while leaving the default checkmark icon, so labeled segments displayed two icons when selected. Labeled segments now use the native icon slot and crossfade between caller icon and checkmark. Icon-only segments follow the [official Compose example](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/components/segmented-button) by keeping the icon in the label slot and giving it an accessible name from `semanticLabels`. Compose retains its selection semantics. See the later accessibility follow-up for the current required-label contract.
- Still to verify on a device: long labels, icon-only segments, selection announcement, RTL, font scaling and caller heights above 40 dp.
- The Android host now follows the real `SingleChoiceSegmentedButtonRow` measured height instead of holding its initial 40 dp reservation when Compose needs more space.
